Actually the classic efficient single-threaded matmul: for i, for k, broadcast A[i][k], then axpy over row j: C[i][j] += A[i][k]*B[k][j]. That's cache-friendly on C and A, sequential B rows. With -O3 vectorization this is decent. Or transpose B and do dot product for each i,j - each dot contiguous in both, vectorized but no reuse of A row. ikj order reuses A element and C row, streams B rows; B is N^2 per i, so B gets read N times total = N^3 traffic, but cache blocking over k and j improves.\n\nFor max performance without external deps, do blocked 3-loop (ikj) with cache blocking and maybe packing. That's a lot of code. A simpler tiled ikj with block sizes tuned gives good performance with autovectorization. Let's implement a tiled version:\n\n```\nfor (i0...) for (k0...) for (j0...) {\n  for (i) {\n    double a_ik = A_local[i*N + k]? \n  }\n}\n```\nBetter standard blocked:\n```\nfor (ii = 0; ii < rows; ii += BM)\n for (kk = 0; kk < N; kk += BK)\n  for (jj = 0; jj < N; jj += BN)\n   for (i=ii; i<...; ++i)\n    for (k=kk; k<...; ++k) {\n      double aik = A[i*N+k];\n      for (j=jj; j<...; ++j)\n        C[i*N+j] += aik * B[k*N+j];\n    }\n```\nThis is the ikj order with j innermost which vectorizes. But the inner loop length BN (e.g., 256) with contiguous B and C. Blocking j, k ensures B block (BK x BN) stays in cache, C block (BM x BN) stays in cache across k. Row start computed accordingly.\n\nImplementation details for tiled matmul:\n\n```\nvoid matrixMultiplyLocal(const double* A, const double* B, double* C,\n                         size_t rows, size_t N) {\n  constexpr size_t BM = 64;\n  constexpr size_t BN = 256; // cache line\n  constexpr size_t BK = 256;\n  // zero C\n  std::fill(C, C + rows*N, 0.0);\n  for (size_t ii=0; ii<rows; ii+=BM)\n    for (size_t kk=0; kk<N; kk+=BK)\n      for (size_t jj=0; jj<N; jj+=BN)\n        for (size_t i=ii; i<min(ii+BM,rows); ++i) {\n          const double* arow = A + i*N;\n          double* crow = C + i*N;\n          for (size_t k=kk; k<min(kk+BK,N); ++k) {\n            const double aik = arow[k];\n            const double* brow = B + k*N;\n            for (size_t j=jj; j<min(jj+BN,N); ++j)\n              crow[j] += aik * brow[j];\n          }\n        }\n}\n```\nWait loop order ii,kk,jj then i,k,j. This means for each (ii,kk,jj) block, we accumulate partial C for that block; C block (BM x BN) may be up to 64*256*8=128KB > L2, but we accumulate it repeatedly for each kk block and jj block. For fixed ii,kk,jj, inner i,k,j: C block reused across k (BK) and B block reused across i (BM), A row block in cache. C block size BM*BN = 128KB, L2 is often 512KB-1MB, fine. But maybe BM=64, BN=128 -> 64KB. Tune BM=64, BN=128, BK=128? Actually the inner loop over j should be a multiple of vector width (4 doubles AVX2, 8 AVX512). BN=128 fine. For each i, k loop over BK and j loop over BN: A row segment and B rows. This is good.\n\nBut even simpler and often very fast: transpose B, then compute dot products with cache-friendly access:\n```\nfor i\n  for j\n    sum = 0\n    for k\n      sum += A[i*N+k] * BT[j*N+k]\n    C[i*N+j]=sum\n```\nBoth A row and BT row contiguous, vectorizable, but A row reused for all j (streaming BT), so B^T streamed rows: total N^3 reads from memory, bandwidth bound.
